# ASTRA AI ISRO Satellite Recommendation Decision Table

This searchable Markdown companion is generated from the uploaded 400-row CSV. Use the table as a decision aid, not as a live operational-status feed. If a requested orbit is unavailable, explain the mismatch and use the listed alternative. Verify critical recommendations against official ISRO sources.

## Recommendation case 1: Communications / Global / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Communications
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no operational communication satellite in LEO, MEO or SSO. (LVM3 has launched 72 OneWeb LEO satellites, but that is a launch service for a commercial operator, not an ISRO satellite.) Switch to GEO for National/Regional.
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: Indian Ocean Region fleet (approx. 48E-93.5E): GSAT-30, GSAT-31, GSAT-24, GSAT-N2, CMS-01, GSAT-9
- Combination members: GSAT-30 + GSAT-31 + GSAT-24 + GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20) + GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite) + CMS-01 (GSAT-12R) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in LEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): GSAT-30: Launched 17 Jan 2020 on Ariane-5 VA-251; about 3,357 kg at 83 E with 12 C-band and 12 Ku-band transponders. Ku covers India; the C-band has extended coverage over the Gulf, many Asian countries and Australia. Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Used for VSAT, DTH, cellular backhaul, ATMs, stock-exchange networks and e-governance. | GSAT-24: Launched 22 Jun 2022 on Ariane-5; about 4,180 kg at 83 E with 24 Ku transponders. First NSIL demand-driven satellite, with all capacity leased for DTH television. | GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20): Launched 19 Nov 2024 on Falcon 9; about 4,700 kg. Ka-band high-throughput payload with 32 beams (8 narrow over the North-East, 24 wide across India) and about 48 Gbps, for broadband, in-flight and maritime connectivity under NSIL. | GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite): Launched 5 May 2017 on GSLV-F09; about 2,230 kg at 48 E with 12 Ku transponders. Provides communication, tele-medicine, disaster support and e-learning to neighbouring countries (not Pakistan). | CMS-01 (GSAT-12R): Launched 17 Dec 2020 on PSLV-C50; about 1,410 kg. Extended C-band services over the Indian mainland, Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands; replaces GSAT-12. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: GEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: GEO
- Feasibility: P (partial)

- Revisit fit: Meets

Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

Ku covers India; the C-band has extended coverage over the Gulf, many Asian countries and Australia. Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Used for VSAT, DTH, cellular backhaul, ATMs, stock-exchange networks and e-governance. | GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite): Launched 5 May 2017 on GSLV-F09; about 2,230 kg at 48 E with 12 Ku transponders. Provides communication, tele-medicine, disaster support and e-learning to neighbouring countries (not Pakistan). | CMS-01 (GSAT-12R): Launched 17 Dec 2020 on PSLV-C50; about 1,410 kg. Extended C-band services over the Indian mainland, Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands; replaces GSAT-12. | GSAT-7 (Rukmini): Launched 30 Aug 2013 on Ariane-5; about 2,650 kg at 74 E. UHF, S, C and Ku payloads giving the Indian Navy secure communications over a footprint of about 2,000 nautical miles across the Indian Ocean Region. | CMS-03 (GSAT-7R): Launched 2 Nov 2025 on LVM3-M5; about 4,410 kg, the heaviest satellite launched from Indian soil. Multi-band payloads giving the Navy secure communications across a wide Indian Ocean footprint. ISRO reports that corrective measures drawn from the NVS-02 anomaly were tested on this satellite. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

- Requested revisit: Daily
- Resolved orbit: GEO
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite, Ku), GSAT-30 (extended-C to Gulf, Asia and Australia), GSAT-31 (Ku), CMS-01 (extended-C over India and islands), GSAT-7 and CMS-03 (Indian Ocean naval comms)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 0.0
- Revisit fit: Meets
- Rationale: Footprints beyond India over South Asia, Middle East, SE Asia and Indian Ocean. Regional sharing of capacity is possible (GSAT-9 was built for SAARC states).
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: GSAT-30 + GSAT-31 + GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite) + CMS-01 (GSAT-12R) + GSAT-7 (Rukmini) + CMS-03 (GSAT-7R)
- Combination details: GSAT-30: Launched 17 Jan 2020 on Ariane-5 VA-251; about 3,357 kg at 83 E with 12 C-band and 12 Ku-band transponders. Ku covers India; the C-band has extended coverage over the Gulf, many Asian countries and Australia. Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Used for VSAT, DTH, cellular backhaul, ATMs, stock-exchange networks and e-governance. | GSAT-9 (South Asia Satellite): Launched 5 May 2017 on GSLV-F09; about 2,230 kg at 48 E with 12 Ku transponders. Provides communication, tele-medicine, disaster support and e-learning to neighbouring countries (not Pakistan). | CMS-01 (GSAT-12R): Launched 17 Dec 2020 on PSLV-C50; about 1,410 kg. Extended C-band services over the Indian mainland, Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands; replaces GSAT-12. | GSAT-7 (Rukmini): Launched 30 Aug 2013 on Ariane-5; about 2,650 kg at 74 E. UHF, S, C and Ku payloads giving the Indian Navy secure communications over a footprint of about 2,000 nautical miles across the Indian Ocean Region. | CMS-03 (GSAT-7R): Launched 2 Nov 2025 on LVM3-M5; about 4,410 kg, the heaviest satellite launched from Indian soil. Multi-band payloads giving the Navy secure communications across a wide Indian Ocean footprint. ISRO reports that corrective measures drawn from the NVS-02 anomaly were tested on this satellite. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

ISRO reports that corrective measures drawn from the NVS-02 anomaly were tested on this satellite. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

- Requested orbit: MEO
- Requested revisit: Weekly
- Resolved orbit: MEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no operational communication satellite in LEO, MEO or SSO. (LVM3 has launched 72 OneWeb LEO satellites, but that is a launch service for a commercial operator, not an ISRO satellite.) Switch to GEO for National/Regional.
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: GSAT-30, GSAT-31, GSAT-24, GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20), CMS-01; defence: GSAT-7, GSAT-7A, CMS-03 (GSAT-7R)
- Combination members: GSAT-30 + GSAT-31 + GSAT-24 + GSAT-11 + GSAT-29 + GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20) + CMS-01 (GSAT-12R) + GSAT-7 (Rukmini) + GSAT-7A (Angrika) + CMS-03 (GSAT-7R) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): GSAT-30: Launched 17 Jan 2020 on Ariane-5 VA-251; about 3,357 kg at 83 E with 12 C-band and 12 Ku-band transponders. Ku covers India; the C-band has extended coverage over the Gulf, many Asian countries and Australia. Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Used for VSAT, DTH, cellular backhaul, ATMs, stock-exchange networks and e-governance. | GSAT-24: Launched 22 Jun 2022 on Ariane-5; about 4,180 kg at 83 E with 24 Ku transponders. First NSIL demand-driven satellite, with all capacity leased for DTH television. | GSAT-11: Launched 5 Dec 2018 on Ariane-5; about 5,854 kg at 74 E. Ku and Ka multi-beam payload (about 32 user beams plus hub beams) with more than 16 Gbps throughput for broadband, rural and island connectivity. | GSAT-29: Launched 14 Nov 2018 on GSLV Mk III-D2; about 3,423 kg. Ka and Ku multi-beam payload, with an optical-communication experiment and a high-resolution camera. Connectivity for remote areas such as Jammu and Kashmir and the North-East. | GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20): Launched 19 Nov 2024 on Falcon 9; about 4,700 kg. Ka-band high-throughput payload with 32 beams (8 narrow over the North-East, 24 wide across India) and about 48 Gbps, for broadband, in-flight and maritime connectivity under NSIL. | CMS-01 (GSAT-12R): Launched 17 Dec 2020 on PSLV-C50; about 1,410 kg. Extended C-band services over the Indian mainland, Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands; replaces GSAT-12. | GSAT-7 (Rukmini): Launched 30 Aug 2013 on Ariane-5; about 2,650 kg at 74 E. UHF, S, C and Ku payloads giving the Indian Navy secure communications over a footprint of about 2,000 nautical miles across the Indian Ocean Region. | GSAT-7A (Angrika): Launched 19 Dec 2018 on GSLV-F11; about 2,250 kg. Ku-band communication for the Indian Air Force, linking ground radar stations, airbases and airborne early-warning aircraft, also supporting Army aviation. | CMS-03 (GSAT-7R): Launched 2 Nov 2025 on LVM3-M5; about 4,410 kg, the heaviest satellite launched from Indian soil. Multi-band payloads giving the Navy secure communications across a wide Indian Ocean footprint. ISRO reports that corrective measures drawn from the NVS-02 anomaly were tested on this satellite. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

- Combination members: GSAT-30 + GSAT-31 + GSAT-24 + GSAT-11 + GSAT-29 + GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20) + CMS-01 (GSAT-12R) + GSAT-7 (Rukmini) + GSAT-7A (Angrika) + CMS-03 (GSAT-7R) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in Sun-synchronous. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): GSAT-30: Launched 17 Jan 2020 on Ariane-5 VA-251; about 3,357 kg at 83 E with 12 C-band and 12 Ku-band transponders. Ku covers India; the C-band has extended coverage over the Gulf, many Asian countries and Australia. Replaces INSAT-4A; used for VSAT, TV uplink, DTH, DSNG and telecom. | GSAT-31: Launched 6 Feb 2019 on Ariane-5; about 2,535 kg at 48 E with 40 Ku-band transponders over India and its islands. Used for VSAT, DTH, cellular backhaul, ATMs, stock-exchange networks and e-governance. | GSAT-24: Launched 22 Jun 2022 on Ariane-5; about 4,180 kg at 83 E with 24 Ku transponders. First NSIL demand-driven satellite, with all capacity leased for DTH television. | GSAT-11: Launched 5 Dec 2018 on Ariane-5; about 5,854 kg at 74 E. Ku and Ka multi-beam payload (about 32 user beams plus hub beams) with more than 16 Gbps throughput for broadband, rural and island connectivity. | GSAT-29: Launched 14 Nov 2018 on GSLV Mk III-D2; about 3,423 kg. Ka and Ku multi-beam payload, with an optical-communication experiment and a high-resolution camera. Connectivity for remote areas such as Jammu and Kashmir and the North-East. | GSAT-N2 (GSAT-20): Launched 19 Nov 2024 on Falcon 9; about 4,700 kg. Ka-band high-throughput payload with 32 beams (8 narrow over the North-East, 24 wide across India) and about 48 Gbps, for broadband, in-flight and maritime connectivity under NSIL. | CMS-01 (GSAT-12R): Launched 17 Dec 2020 on PSLV-C50; about 1,410 kg. Extended C-band services over the Indian mainland, Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands; replaces GSAT-12. | GSAT-7 (Rukmini): Launched 30 Aug 2013 on Ariane-5; about 2,650 kg at 74 E. UHF, S, C and Ku payloads giving the Indian Navy secure communications over a footprint of about 2,000 nautical miles across the Indian Ocean Region. | GSAT-7A (Angrika): Launched 19 Dec 2018 on GSLV-F11; about 2,250 kg. Ku-band communication for the Indian Air Force, linking ground radar stations, airbases and airborne early-warning aircraft, also supporting Army aviation. | CMS-03 (GSAT-7R): Launched 2 Nov 2025 on LVM3-M5; about 4,410 kg, the heaviest satellite launched from Indian soil. Multi-band payloads giving the Navy secure communications across a wide Indian Ocean footprint. ISRO reports that corrective measures drawn from the NVS-02 anomaly were tested on this satellite. | Together: Ku and C-band satellites carry TV, VSAT and telecom traffic; Ka-band high-throughput satellites (GSAT-11, GSAT-29, GSAT-N2) add broadband capacity; naval and air-force satellites add secure links. Overlapping slots between 48 E and 93.5 E give redundancy, load sharing and backup if one satellite is lost. Global scope is Partial because this covers only the Indian Ocean sector.

- Combination members: 
- Combination details: No ISRO satellite combination exists for this request.

## Recommendation case 81: Earth Observation (Optical) / Global / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: P (partial)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Cartosat-3 (~0.28 m PAN), Cartosat-2D/2E/2F (~0.65 m), Resourcesat-2A (LISS-4 5.8 m, LISS-3 23.5 m, AWiFS 56 m), HySIS (hyperspectral), Oceansat-3 (ocean colour)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 120.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: Same limits as SSO Global; EOS-08 is inclined (~37 deg) and cannot reach high latitudes.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06)
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: The SSO fleet above plus EOS-08, whose infrared imager adds night-time and thermal views (fires, heat, volcanoes) over the tropics. EOS-08 cannot support Polar or Global requests.

## Recommendation case 85: Earth Observation (Optical) / Global / MEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: MEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: MEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no MEO imaging satellite. Use SSO (resolution), LEO, or GEO (cadence).
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: INSAT-3DS/3DR/3D (coarse full-disk, Indian Ocean sector only)
- Combination members: INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

## Recommendation case 89: Earth Observation (Optical) / Global / GEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: GEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: GEO
- Feasibility: P (partial)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: INSAT-3DS/3DR/3D (coarse full-disk, Indian Ocean sector only)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 0.5
- Revisit fit: Partially meets
- Rationale: A single geostationary sector - roughly one-third of the globe - only at coarse resolution.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D)
- Combination details: INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

## Recommendation case 101: Earth Observation (Optical) / Regional / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Regional

- Requested revisit: Continuous

- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06) + EOS-08
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | EOS-08: Launched 16 Aug 2024 on SSLV-D3; a 175 kg microsatellite in a 475 km orbit inclined about 37 degrees. Payloads: EOIR (visible, mid-wave and long-wave infrared for day and night imaging, fire and volcano monitoring), GNSS-R (ocean wind, soil moisture, flood inundation) and an ultraviolet dosimeter supporting Gaganyaan. Limits: small satellite, restricted to about +/-40 degrees latitude. | Together: The SSO fleet above plus EOS-08, whose infrared imager adds night-time and thermal views (fires, heat, volcanoes) over the tropics. EOS-08 cannot support Polar or Global requests.

## Recommendation case 105: Earth Observation (Optical) / Regional / MEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Regional
- Requested orbit: MEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: MEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no MEO imaging satellite. Use SSO (resolution), LEO, or GEO (cadence).
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A, commissioning); INSAT-3DS/3DR/3D (coarse)
- Combination members: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A) + INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): EOS-05 (GISAT-1A): 2,367 kg, I-2K bus. Launched 4 Sep 2026 on GSLV-F17, replacing the failed GISAT-1/EOS-03 of 2021, and reached geosynchronous orbit around 8 Sep 2026. Designed to image India and surroundings repeatedly from about 36,000 km in multispectral and hyperspectral bands, for disasters, weather and cloud, agriculture and forest monitoring. Status: in commissioning, so treat as Partial; resolution is coarser than LEO satellites. | INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

## Recommendation case 113: Earth Observation (Optical) / Regional / Sun-synchronous / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: Regional
- Requested orbit: Sun-synchronous
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: Sun-synchronous
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Cartosat-3 (~0.28 m PAN), Cartosat-2D/2E/2F (~0.65 m), Resourcesat-2A (LISS-4 5.8 m, LISS-3 23.5 m, AWiFS 56 m), HySIS (hyperspectral), Oceansat-3 (ocean colour)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 48.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: On-demand tasking over neighbouring countries; systematic multispectral mapping with Resourcesat-2A.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06)
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

## Recommendation case 121: Earth Observation (Optical) / National (India) / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Cartosat-3 (~0.28 m PAN), Cartosat-2D/2E/2F (~0.65 m), Resourcesat-2A (LISS-4 5.8 m, LISS-3 23.5 m, AWiFS 56 m), HySIS (hyperspectral), Oceansat-3 (ocean colour); EOS-08 (EOIR)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 24.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: LEO includes SSO; EOS-08 adds mid-wave/long-wave infrared imaging including night-time thermal.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06) + EOS-08
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | EOS-08: Launched 16 Aug 2024 on SSLV-D3; a 175 kg microsatellite in a 475 km orbit inclined about 37 degrees. Payloads: EOIR (visible, mid-wave and long-wave infrared for day and night imaging, fire and volcano monitoring), GNSS-R (ocean wind, soil moisture, flood inundation) and an ultraviolet dosimeter supporting Gaganyaan. Limits: small satellite, restricted to about +/-40 degrees latitude. | Together: The SSO fleet above plus EOS-08, whose infrared imager adds night-time and thermal views (fires, heat, volcanoes) over the tropics. EOS-08 cannot support Polar or Global requests.

## Recommendation case 125: Earth Observation (Optical) / National (India) / MEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: MEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: MEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no MEO imaging satellite. Use SSO (resolution), LEO, or GEO (cadence).
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A) - India's first geosynchronous imager, launched 4 Sep 2026, in GEO since ~8 Sep 2026, commissioning; INSAT-3DS/3DR/3D imagers (~1 km) as coarse optical now
- Combination members: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A) + INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): EOS-05 (GISAT-1A): 2,367 kg, I-2K bus. Launched 4 Sep 2026 on GSLV-F17, replacing the failed GISAT-1/EOS-03 of 2021, and reached geosynchronous orbit around 8 Sep 2026. Designed to image India and surroundings repeatedly from about 36,000 km in multispectral and hyperspectral bands, for disasters, weather and cloud, agriculture and forest monitoring. Status: in commissioning, so treat as Partial; resolution is coarser than LEO satellites. | INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

## Recommendation case 129: Earth Observation (Optical) / National (India) / GEO / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: GEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: GEO
- Feasibility: P (partial)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A) - India's first geosynchronous imager, launched 4 Sep 2026, in GEO since ~8 Sep 2026, commissioning; INSAT-3DS/3DR/3D imagers (~1 km) as coarse optical now
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 0.25
- Revisit fit: Meets
- Rationale: Staring/sector imaging of India at minutes-scale cadence is the design purpose of EOS-05 (multispectral plus hyperspectral VNIR/SWIR). Treat as Partial until ISRO declares it operational.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A) + INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D)
- Combination details: EOS-05 (GISAT-1A): 2,367 kg, I-2K bus. Launched 4 Sep 2026 on GSLV-F17, replacing the failed GISAT-1/EOS-03 of 2021, and reached geosynchronous orbit around 8 Sep 2026. Designed to image India and surroundings repeatedly from about 36,000 km in multispectral and hyperspectral bands, for disasters, weather and cloud, agriculture and forest monitoring. Status: in commissioning, so treat as Partial; resolution is coarser than LEO satellites. | INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT imagers give coarse views every 15 to 30 minutes and EOS-05 adds finer multispectral and hyperspectral detail once operational. Use GEO to detect fast-changing events such as cyclones, floods and fires, then task SSO satellites for high-resolution follow-up.

## Recommendation case 133: Earth Observation (Optical) / National (India) / Sun-synchronous / Continuous
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: Sun-synchronous
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: Sun-synchronous
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Cartosat-3 (~0.28 m PAN), Cartosat-2D/2E/2F (~0.65 m), Resourcesat-2A (LISS-4 5.8 m, LISS-3 23.5 m, AWiFS 56 m), HySIS (hyperspectral), Oceansat-3 (ocean colour)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 24.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: Very high resolution for cartography, urban planning, defence; multispectral for crops/forests/water. Daily access is achievable only for tasked small areas via off-nadir pointing across several satellites; systematic wide-area coverage is ~5 days (AWiFS), ~2 days (OCM).
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06)
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

## Recommendation case 139: Earth Observation (Optical) / National (India) / Let ASTRAAI decide / Daily
- Application: Earth Observation (Optical)
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: Let ASTRAAI decide
- Requested revisit: Daily
- Resolved orbit: Sun-synchronous
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Cartosat-3 (~0.28 m PAN), Cartosat-2D/2E/2F (~0.65 m), Resourcesat-2A (LISS-4 5.8 m, LISS-3 23.5 m, AWiFS 56 m), HySIS (hyperspectral), Oceansat-3 (ocean colour)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 24.0
- Revisit fit: Meets
- Rationale: Very high resolution for cartography, urban planning, defence; multispectral for crops/forests/water. Daily access is achievable only for tasked small areas via off-nadir pointing across several satellites; systematic wide-area coverage is ~5 days (AWiFS), ~2 days (OCM).
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Cartosat-3 + Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A) + Resourcesat-2 / 2A + HySIS + Oceansat-3 (EOS-06)
- Combination details: Cartosat-3: Third-generation Cartosat launched 27 Nov 2019 on PSLV-C47 into a sun-synchronous orbit at about 509 km. Panchromatic imaging at about 0.25 to 0.28 m (ISRO's finest) plus multispectral bands, over a swath of roughly 16 km, with agile pointing for off-nadir, stereo and rapid re-targeting. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. Limits: cloud and darkness, narrow swath. | Resourcesat-2 / 2A: Resourcesat-2 (2011) and Resourcesat-2A (Dec 2016, the one listed operational) fly at about 817 km in SSO with three cameras: LISS-4 (5.8 m, steerable), LISS-3 (23.5 m, includes shortwave infrared) and AWiFS (56 m, about 740 km swath, about 5-day repeat). Best for crop acreage, forest cover, water bodies and land use. Limits: moderate resolution, cloud cover. | HySIS: Hyperspectral Imaging Satellite launched 29 Nov 2018 on PSLV-C43, SSO about 636 km. Records about 55 contiguous bands across visible-near-infrared and shortwave infrared at about 30 m, which allows identification of minerals, crop stress, vegetation type and coastal conditions. Limits: modest resolution and swath, limited tasking, large data volumes. | Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Use wide-to-narrow cueing: wide-swath Resourcesat-2A (740 km AWiFS) and Oceansat-3 detect change, Cartosat-3 and the Cartosat-2 series zoom in at sub-metre detail, and HySIS identifies materials. Several agile satellites tasked together shorten the revisit over a target.

- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Best for city-scale mapping, infrastructure, change detection and defence. Limits: narrow swath, daylight and clear sky only, tasked rather than systematic. | Cartosat-2 series (2D, 2E, 2F, 2A): Cartosat-2D (Feb 2017), 2E (Jun 2017) and 2F (Jan 2018) are agile SSO satellites at about 500 km with about 0.65 m panchromatic imaging and a swath near 10 km; Cartosat-2A (2008, about 1 m class) is listed operational but ageing. Several satellites tasked together raise the revisit over a target. Best for cartography, utilities, urban growth and cadastral work. ## Recommendation case 161: Synthetic Aperture Radar / Global / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: P (partial)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: NISAR, RISAT-1A (global); RISAT-2B family (about +/-45 deg latitude only)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 144.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: Only NISAR/RISAT-1A provide beyond-tropics coverage.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

- Rationale: Only NISAR/RISAT-1A provide beyond-tropics coverage.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

## Recommendation case 165: Synthetic Aperture Radar / Global / MEO / Continuous
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: Global

- Requested revisit: Continuous

- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: No SAR in GEO or MEO exists at ISRO. For continuous all-weather monitoring use LEO/SSO SAR plus GEO optical/IR (INSAT-3DS, EOS-05) for cloud-free periods.
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to Sun-synchronous: NISAR (global land/ice systematic), RISAT-1A
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) (in Sun-synchronous)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (Sun-synchronous): NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | Together: NISAR gives systematic L-band and S-band maps (forest biomass, soil moisture, deformation, ice) and RISAT-1A adds C-band. The different wavelengths respond to different surface properties, so combining them improves classification of vegetation, flooding and ground movement.

- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | Together: NISAR gives systematic L-band and S-band maps (forest biomass, soil moisture, deformation, ice) and RISAT-1A adds C-band. The different wavelengths respond to different surface properties, so combining them improves classification of vegetation, flooding and ground movement.

## Recommendation case 181: Synthetic Aperture Radar / Regional / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: Regional
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: RISAT-2B, RISAT-2BR1, NISAR
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 48.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: Inclined X-band satellites cover South Asia and the tropics well.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | Together: NISAR gives systematic L-band and S-band maps (forest biomass, soil moisture, deformation, ice) and RISAT-1A adds C-band. The different wavelengths respond to different surface properties, so combining them improves classification of vegetation, flooding and ground movement.

## Recommendation case 191: Synthetic Aperture Radar / Regional / GEO / Daily
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: Regional
- Requested orbit: GEO
- Requested revisit: Daily
- Resolved orbit: GEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: No SAR in GEO or MEO exists at ISRO. For continuous all-weather monitoring use LEO/SSO SAR plus GEO optical/IR (INSAT-3DS, EOS-05) for cloud-free periods.
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to LEO: RISAT-2B, RISAT-2BR1, NISAR
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01 (in LEO)
- Combination details: Not available in GEO. Nearest ISRO combination (LEO): NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

## Recommendation case 204: Synthetic Aperture Radar / National (India) / LEO / Weekly
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Weekly

- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: RISAT-2B, RISAT-2BR1 (X-band, ~37 deg incl.), EOS-01 (RISAT-2BR2, verify status), plus NISAR and RISAT-1A
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 24.0
- Revisit fit: Meets
- Rationale: X-band spotlight/strip up to sub-metre class for defence, border and disaster work; inclined orbits give repeated passes over India. Revisit is tasking-based, not systematic.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. - Recommended ISRO satellites:

- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to Sun-synchronous: NISAR (NASA-ISRO, L-band + S-band, 747 km, 12-day exact repeat), RISAT-1A / EOS-04 (C-band, ~529 km)
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) (in Sun-synchronous)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (Sun-synchronous): NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | Together: NISAR gives systematic L-band and S-band maps (forest biomass, soil moisture, deformation, ice) and RISAT-1A adds C-band. The different wavelengths respond to different surface properties, so combining them improves classification of vegetation, flooding and ground movement.

- Rationale: NISAR gives systematic, all-weather, day/night imaging with 242 km swath and 3-10 m resolution; asc+desc sampling about every 6 days. RISAT-1A adds C-band (~3-50 m modes, 25-day repeat).
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04)
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | Together: NISAR gives systematic L-band and S-band maps (forest biomass, soil moisture, deformation, ice) and RISAT-1A adds C-band. The different wavelengths respond to different surface properties, so combining them improves classification of vegetation, flooding and ground movement.

## Recommendation case 219: Synthetic Aperture Radar / National (India) / Let ASTRAAI decide / Daily
- Application: Synthetic Aperture Radar
- Coverage: National (India)
- Requested orbit: Let ASTRAAI decide
- Requested revisit: Daily
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: RISAT-2B, RISAT-2BR1 (X-band, ~37 deg incl.), EOS-01 (RISAT-2BR2, verify status), plus NISAR and RISAT-1A
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 24.0
- Revisit fit: Meets
- Rationale: X-band spotlight/strip up to sub-metre class for defence, border and disaster work; inclined orbits give repeated passes over India. Revisit is tasking-based, not systematic.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: NISAR + RISAT-1A (EOS-04) + RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01
- Combination details: NISAR: NASA-ISRO radar observatory of about 2,400 kg launched 30 Jul 2025 on GSLV-F16, operational since 7 Nov 2025. Orbit about 747 km, 98.4 degrees, 6 am / 6 pm crossing. NASA supplies the L-band SAR (24 cm) and a 12 m mesh reflector; ISRO supplies the S-band SAR (10 cm), the satellite bus and the launch. SweepSAR gives a 242 km swath at 3 to 10 m with an exact 12-day repeat, in single, dual and quad polarisation; data are free and open. Best for biomass, soil moisture, ground deformation, glaciers, sea ice, floods and crops. Limits: systematic rather than on demand, no daily revisit. | RISAT-1A (EOS-04): Launched 14 Feb 2022 on PSLV-C52; about 1,700 kg, SSO about 529 km. C-band SAR with several modes from a few metres to about 50 m resolution and a wide swath in coarse mode; about 25-day repeat. Best for crops, soil moisture, flood mapping and forestry. Limits: coarser than X-band, slow repeat. Its sister RISAT-1B (EOS-09) was lost in the May 2025 PSLV-C61 failure. | RISAT-2B / 2BR1 / EOS-01: RISAT-2B (May 2019), RISAT-2BR1 (Dec 2019) and EOS-01 / RISAT-2BR2 (Nov 2020, status to verify) carry X-band SAR on a 3.6 m radial-rib antenna in LEO inclined about 37 degrees at roughly 550 to 580 km. Spotlight, strip and wider modes reach sub-metre to a few metres. The inclined orbit gives repeated passes over India and the tropics, so this is the fastest-revisit SAR family, but it does not reach polar latitudes. Best for defence, borders, ship detection, floods and disaster response. | Together: The X-band RISAT-2B family provides fast, high-resolution tasked views of India (minimum revisit about daily), while NISAR adds systematic wide-area L and S-band coverage. For Polar and Global requests only the SSO members (NISAR, RISAT-1A) apply.

## Recommendation case 261: Navigation / Regional / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Navigation
- Coverage: Regional
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: NavIC uses GEO and inclined-GEO (IGSO, 29 deg) orbits, not MEO/LEO/SSO. ISRO has no LEO or MEO navigation satellite. Switch to GEO (NavIC).
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: NavIC (same satellites as National) - extended service area approx. 30S-50N, 30E-130E
- Combination members: NavIC (IRNSS-1B, 1I, NVS-01) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in LEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): NavIC (IRNSS-1B, 1I, NVS-01): India's regional navigation system: a base layer of 3 GEO and 4 IGSO satellites broadcasting L5 (1176.45 MHz) and S-band signals, with an open Standard Positioning Service and an encrypted Restricted Service. Primary area India plus about 1,500 km; extended area about 30 S to 50 N and 30 E to 130 E. Second-generation NVS satellites add an L1 signal for interoperability with GPS and Galileo and indigenous rubidium clocks. Status Oct 2026: IRNSS-1B, IRNSS-1I and NVS-01 fully operational for positioning; five older satellites messaging only; NVS-02 stranded; NVS-03 awaiting launch. | Together: NavIC GEO and IGSO satellites supply position and time over India and the surrounding region; GAGAN adds corrections and integrity data for aviation; the partly working older satellites still carry messaging and disaster alerts. Capability is reduced until NVS-03 and later satellites are launched.

Second-generation NVS satellites add an L1 signal for interoperability with GPS and Galileo and indigenous rubidium clocks. Status Oct 2026: IRNSS-1B, IRNSS-1I and NVS-01 fully operational for positioning; five older satellites messaging only; NVS-02 stranded; NVS-03 awaiting launch. | Together: NavIC GEO and IGSO satellites supply position and time over India and the surrounding region; GAGAN adds corrections and integrity data for aviation; the partly working older satellites still carry messaging and disaster alerts. Capability is reduced until NVS-03 and later satellites are launched.

- Combination details: Not available in LEO. GEO payloads on GSAT-8, GSAT-10 and GSAT-15 broadcast corrections and integrity data so aircraft can use GPS for precision approaches in Indian airspace. - Combination members: NavIC (IRNSS-1B, 1I, NVS-01) + GAGAN
- Combination details: NavIC (IRNSS-1B, 1I, NVS-01): India's regional navigation system: a base layer of 3 GEO and 4 IGSO satellites broadcasting L5 (1176.45 MHz) and S-band signals, with an open Standard Positioning Service and an encrypted Restricted Service. Primary area India plus about 1,500 km; extended area about 30 S to 50 N and 30 E to 130 E. Second-generation NVS satellites add an L1 signal for interoperability with GPS and Galileo and indigenous rubidium clocks. Status Oct 2026: IRNSS-1B, IRNSS-1I and NVS-01 fully operational for positioning; five older satellites messaging only; NVS-02 stranded; NVS-03 awaiting launch. | GAGAN: GPS Aided GEO Augmented Navigation, built by ISRO and the Airports Authority of India. GEO payloads on GSAT-8, GSAT-10 and GSAT-15 broadcast corrections and integrity data so aircraft can use GPS for precision approaches in Indian airspace. Depends on GPS signals; verify which GSAT payloads are currently active. | Together: NavIC GEO and IGSO satellites supply position and time over India and the surrounding region; GAGAN adds corrections and integrity data for aviation; the partly working older satellites still carry messaging and disaster alerts. Capability is reduced until NVS-03 and later satellites are launched.

## Recommendation case 321: Meteorological / Global / LEO / Continuous
- Application: Meteorological
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: LEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: LEO
- Feasibility: N (native)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: Oceansat-3 (SSO)
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 48.0
- Revisit fit: Does not meet
- Rationale: Global ocean coverage comes from Oceansat-3 (SSO); EOS-08 only covers within about +/-40 deg latitude.
- Alternative if unavailable: 
- Combination members: Oceansat-3 (EOS-06)
- Combination details: Oceansat-3 (EOS-06): Launched 26 Nov 2022 on PSLV-C54, SSO about 720 km. Carries OCM-3 (13-band ocean colour with a very wide swath of over 1,000 km), SSTM (thermal sea-surface temperature), SCAT-3 (Ku-band scatterometer for ocean-surface wind) and ARGOS (data collection). Gives about 2-day coverage. Best for chlorophyll, algal blooms, fishery advisories, cyclone winds and sea-surface temperature. Limits: coarse over land. | Together: Polar Oceansat-3 and the tropical, inclined EOS-08 sample winds, sea state, sea-surface temperature and soil moisture at different times and latitudes, improving coverage over India and the Indian Ocean. EOS-08 is not used for Global or Polar requests.

## Recommendation case 325: Meteorological / Global / MEO / Continuous
- Application: Meteorological
- Coverage: Global
- Requested orbit: MEO
- Requested revisit: Continuous
- Resolved orbit: MEO
- Feasibility: X (not available)
- Recommended ISRO satellites: 
- Best achievable revisit (hours): 
- Revisit fit: 
- Rationale: ISRO has no MEO weather satellite. Switch to GEO (continuous/hourly) or SSO (daily/polar).
- Alternative if unavailable: Switch orbit to GEO: INSAT-3DS, INSAT-3DR, INSAT-3D
- Combination members: INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D) (in GEO)
- Combination details: Not available in MEO. Nearest ISRO combination (GEO): INSAT-3D family (3DS, 3DR, 3D): INSAT-3D (Jul 2013), INSAT-3DR (Sep 2016, 74 E) and INSAT-3DS (Feb 2024, GSLV-F14) are ISRO's geostationary weather satellites at about 74 E to 82 E. Each has a 6-channel Imager (visible and shortwave infrared at about 1 km, mid and thermal infrared at about 4 km, water vapour at about 8 km); 3D and 3DR also carry a 19-channel Sounder for temperature and humidity profiles, plus data-relay and search-and-rescue payloads. Full disk about every 30 minutes with faster sector scans. Limits: cannot see the poles, coarse resolution. | Together: INSAT-3DS is the primary imager; INSAT-3DR and INSAT-3D at 74 E and 82 E give backup, cross-checks of cloud winds and extra sounder profiles. Together they deliver continuous cyclone, monsoon, fog and rainfall monitoring of the Indian Ocean Region.
